Comment:
Tell me how I want you to. I want you to help me fix a couple things, because there's "enough for what?" What is "enough for what?" Think about that. It has to be contextually sensible. It's not just "enough for what" or guaranteed; it has to be used in a sentence. We want to fix that. Look at this. I'm not just using the word "enough" repeatedly. You should use other terms synonymously, and it should make sense in the context. Just saying "enough for what" doesn't make sense.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. These are source-backed authoring recommendations, not a new approved lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Selected HTML: [original source](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), lines 156-170, supplies explanation depth and presence/absence sequence. Novelty must not obscure the condition or require untaught knowledge. |\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the parcel example, three possible cases distinguish the permitted relationship from an invalid reverse:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Blue sticker and inspected: fits the rule.\\r\\n- Inspected without a blue sticker: also fits the rule.\\r\\n- Blue sticker without inspection: breaks the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule does not itself say any such parcel exists. Examples of possible cases illustrate consistency, not existence claims. This distinction matters when moving from individual facts to general rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Calibration examples for different LSAT surfaces\\r\\n\\r\\nAll examples below are original drafts for this reference. They demonstrate writing mechanisms; they are not assigned work or approved curriculum.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain sufficient and necessary without rushing\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose yours has a blue sticker. That\'s enough to know it has been inspected. You don\'t need another fact about the parcel. The rule has already told you what must be true of every parcel with that sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We call the blue sticker the **sufficient condition**. Sufficient means enough. Here, enough to establish inspection under this rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Inspection is the **necessary condition**. A blue-sticker parcel has to meet that requirement. Otherwise it would be an exception to a rule that allows no exceptions.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now suppose a parcel has been inspected. Does it have to have a blue sticker?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We still don\'t know. Inspection was required for the blue-sticker parcels. The shop never said those were the only parcels it inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis returns to meaning at every term. The example does not assert why the shop uses stickers or when they are applied. Those extra facts are unnecessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Teach a necessary expression through its whole meaning\\r\\n\\r\\n> Editing this newsletter requires an account.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> What does the sentence say the editor needs? An account. Keep that complete idea on the required side of the relationship:\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Edits this newsletter → Has an account**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn\'t told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give a flashcard a reasoning task\\r\\n\\r\\n**Front**\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Back**\\r\\n\\r\\n> No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn\'t say every scanned package goes there.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Make feedback identify the missed connection\\r\\n\\r\\n> You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn\'t said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain an ordinary learning objection\\r\\n\\r\\n> “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”\\r\\n>\\r\\n> With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You don\'t have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Revise the draft in a useful order\\r\\n\\r\\n### First, check the reasoning and scope\\r\\n\\r\\nList what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a rewrite, preserve Joshua\'s established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, check the learner\'s experience\\r\\n\\r\\nRead as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?\\r\\n\\r\\nPut the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, listen to the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nRead aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let\'s,” or “That\'s it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Finally, verify the student surface\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor\'s planning map separate from what the learner sees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing\\r\\n\\r\\nScore each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Dimension | What a strong draft does |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |\\r\\n| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |\\r\\n| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |\\r\\n| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |\\r\\n| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |\\r\\n| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |\\r\\n| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |\\r\\n| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |\\r\\n| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |\\r\\n| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |\\r\\n| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |\\r\\n| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua\'s response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.\\r\\n\\r\\n## A reusable writing brief\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nWrite this LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice.\\r\\nRead wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,\\r\\nand wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of\\r\\nthe idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.\\r\\nShow each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner\\r\\nwould actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave\\r\\nroom for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\nVary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short\\r\\nsentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.\\r\\nTeach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.\\r\\nRepeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner\'s view.\\r\\nShow a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve Joshua\'s method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,\\r\\nand the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.\\r\\nDo not copy the reference\'s examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural\\r\\ncounts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,\\r\\nor decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing\\r\\nprompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought\\r\\nfollows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Keep this reference current\\r\\n\\r\\nThis file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.\\r\\n33:2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same relationship with if at the beginning and in the middle, and show necessary targets both before and after requirement language. Neither sentence position nor the words immediately following the indicator determine placement. Preserve if versus only if as different complete expressions.\\r\\n35:A rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.\\r\\n39:Joshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\n43:Joshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Two connecting rules produce another conditional rule without establishing a case exists. Adding a fact to the chain is a separate step. Explain all four fact situations and a hidden chain revealed by contraposition rather than leaving them implicit in an interactive model. Rules include general logical relationships, not only policies or obligations.\\r\\n47:Joshua rejected the recap\'s small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.\\r\\n210:**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”\\r\\n213:**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional\\r\\n246:blurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\n256:Joshua requested the Some, Most, and All note as a teaching-flow and visual-quality reference. Quantity examples and exercises do not belong in the separate facts/rules note. Remove that off-topic material; retain the distinction between what a rule says and what a fact establishes. Before future recaps, read the accumulated corrections in the Post Session Recap skill\'s `references/recap-calibration.md`. Preserve source-driven scope while matching the reference\'s teaching quality.\\r\\n262:The revised local copy asks what the learner needs to decide: “Who does the rule say will receive a wristband?”, “What kind of key is needed to open the cabinet?”, and “What kind of pass does entry require?” The lesson demonstrates why before noon, current, registered and with yellow tags matter. Dropping one of those words changes which cases the rule covers. An arrow is checked by imagining its left condition and asking what the sentence then establishes, rather than treating the first English clause as the left side.\\r\\n264:Joshua also asked for the mixed drill to train a skill and build a habit, with one question visible and a progress bar. The current local response uses five stages with a concrete purpose for each: retain the full conditions, build a diagram, attempt a translation before supplied labels, compare meanings, then transfer to unfamiliar cases. Error feedback distinguishes dropped qualifications, reversed roles, required versus enough and changed meaning. A fresh question checks the repaired distinction; correcting the exposed answer alone does not demonstrate transfer. Written translations are compared by the learner and are not represented as automatically graded.\\r\\n266:Keep modality in the examples. An electric vehicle that is denied permission disproves the claim that being electric guarantees permission; a vehicle merely staying outside does not. A required action is an obligation, not a fact proving compliance. Confirmation practice for that mistake must test another obligation rather than an unrelated wording comparison.\\r\\n270:Further steering in the same session: “Keep the complete condition” was itself awkward as a visible heading. Ask who or what the particular rule covers instead. Joshua also rejected the landing page\'s total question/stage counts, per-stage counts, long stage list and padded explanation of repairs. A shorter launch should explain the immediate task; each stage should introduce its purpose and instructions when the learner reaches it. That latest flow revision was requested while final verification was still pending, so this record does not certify its implementation.\\r\\n275:Joshua rejected Inspect set as unclear and awkward. Use Preview drill for viewing the exact selected passages and questions before starting. Keep Start this drill distinct and explain whether answers are hidden and whether work is saved. Generic Menu is also insufficient where the actual action is Question details. This correction does not justify adding more launch instructions, repeated summaries, or unrelated assigned work inside RC setup.\\n"}}'}]

Required-on-right meaning is emphasized in the adjacent 9:49 transcript, beyond memorization. |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Keyword inventory is now observed, with scope limits\\r\\n\\r\\nThe last board\'s **left** inventory is: if, when, whenever, the only; all, each, any, every, no. Its **right** inventory is: ensures, implies, leads to, will, always; only, only if, requires, must, necessitates, depends on, relies on, unless, until, without. These are the vendor\'s observed labels, not an unconditional parsing algorithm and not Joshua\'s complete group taxonomy.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the ordinary Group 1/2 work, adapt the rule/universal and requirement/guarantee meanings, preserving the full qualified idea attached to an expression. Sentence location does not determine diagram side. In an if sentence, the if-clause may come first or last. In a requirement sentence, the goal can come before the required thing or after it. Teach students to identify what is enough and what that situation requires, then read the whole relationship back.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep **no** and **unless/until/without** in their separately taught negative/exception lessons. Their appearance in the reference does not eliminate the additional transformations. **Only** and **the only** require separate grammatical explanations. **Always**, **will**, and **must** must be read within the actual sentence: a rule prescribing an action is not proof that someone complied. The source\'s equivalent-expression board makes these distinctions worth teaching with controlled contrasts.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to adapt and what to correct\\r\\n\\r\\nUse original examples and wording. Useful design patterns are: stable rule plus one changed fact; full-idea labels; prompt before reveal; role labels next to terms; original and contrapositive side by side after the meaning is understood; equivalent sentences before a reference table; and a final meaning-based check rather than keyword recitation alone.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not copy the clip\'s introductory numerical prevalence claims into course copy. No supporting study is supplied. Do not repeat the caption\'s statement that the rule disappears when the sufficient condition is absent: the rule still holds, but that fact alone leaves the other condition unsettled. Do not imply this ten-minute overview proves mastery or teaches all conditional skills.\\r\\n\\r\\nNo fresh real-time video watch or audio review occurred in this ingest. Necessary.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# A conditional rule does not say its trigger occurred\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua supplied this LSAT Demon clip and requested its integration into the Conditional Bootcamp on 2026-10-03. The complete supplied transcript, 0:04 to 5:17, was read. The speaker is not identified in the clip. The [video URL](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_ho_yxw_Qqc) is present, but no fresh playback or visual-board inspection was performed. There are no local image references to inspect.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Preserved evidence\\r\\n\\r\\n- [Exact Markdown clip](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/LSAT%20Demon%20%20Understanding%20Sufficient%20v.%20Necessary.md), 3,854 bytes.\\r\\n- [Provenance](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/PROVENANCE.md) and [hash manifest](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS).\\r\\n- SHA-256: `1fa38af6382cbc3f77efe9785499ef5416f0c4eabfcc074370a7bb6c05ebdb7f`.\\r\\n- Publication and capture dates unknown. Inbox last-write date was 2026-10-03. Archived and ingested 2026-10-03. Original retained unchanged; no matching archived hash or completed source summary found before intake.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What the source teaches\\r\\n\\r\\n| Timestamp | Source content, paraphrased | Useful teaching function |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 0:04–0:26 | A conditional is a rule qualified by a condition, and several phrasings may state the same relationship. | Define the relationship before teaching vocabulary. |\\r\\n| 0:51–1:31 | The speaker says a dropped pen will fall, then asks whether that establishes it will fall for sure. The pen has not been dropped. | Separate a hypothetical rule from a fact that satisfies it. |\\r\\n| 1:48–2:15 | Sufficient means enough to establish the other condition. Necessary is what must hold when the sufficient condition holds. | Give each technical term an ordinary meaning and a stable example. |\\r\\n| 2:40–2:59 | Sufficient belongs left and necessary right in the arrow representation. | Introduce notation only after explaining the roles. |\\r\\n| 3:35–5:17 | If the pen did not fall, it could not have been dropped under the stated rule. The source calls this the contrapositive and emphasizes equivalence. | Ask for the consequence of a missing requirement before naming the operation. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe only explicit opening question asks whether the rule itself establishes that the pen will fall. Later reasoning is mainly a narrated contrapositive demonstration, not a supplied exercise bank. Do not claim the clip includes multiple-choice questions or a reviewed answer key.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How this belongs in Joshua\'s lesson\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [existing 13-part source map](../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) still controls progression. This source supports deeper examples in the rule/fact, sufficient, necessary and conceptual-contrapositive sections. It does not replace the skeleton or Joshua\'s five construction steps. Use the [selected teaching voice](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md).\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal adaptation: **If a display lamp is lit, its battery is installed.** First give only the rule and ask whether it establishes that the lamp is lit. Then add the fact that it is lit and ask what follows. Later, after explaining the necessary role, ask what follows if the battery is absent. Keep the same lamp and the same stated rule so the changing fact is the learner\'s focus.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is an authored hypothetical, not a technical claim about every real lamp. Introduce it explicitly as the rule for this exercise. Use fresh objects for independent practice after the walkthrough.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Boundaries and corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n- The source\'s phrasing that the rule is not “hard and fast” could suggest exceptions. Our conditional has no exception when its sufficient condition holds; what is conditional is its application to a case.\\r\\n- A trigger need not cause an event, and a conditional need not describe a time sequence. The arrow means implication under the stated rule.\\r\\n- Remove the source\'s weapon analogy and dismissive comparisons with schoolchildren. A learner\'s uncertainty deserves an explanation.\\r\\n- The missing sufficient condition does not remove the rule. It leaves the other condition unsettled by that fact and rule alone.\\r\\n- The clip is evidence of a teaching approach, not comparative learning-science evidence or proof of student outcomes.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [abstract-conditionals continuation](lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md), [LSAT Lab supplement](lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md), [selected voice source](conditional-reasoning-voice.md).\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"41fdfa","wall_time_seconds":0.4648117,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1738,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: source\\r\\nsubject: LSAT Demon Mastering Conditional Statements\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-03\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\ntags: [lsat, conditionals, pedagogy]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/Mastering Conditional Statements.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# One sufficient route need not be the only route\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua requested all clipped LSAT Demon conditional material for intuitive original examples. This generic-title article was found after the two explicitly named Demon clips. Unlike those transcripts, it contains a six-question quiz with a supplied answer key. The entire supplied article and answer key were read on 2026-10-03.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Preserved evidence\\r\\n\\r\\n- [Exact clip](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/Mastering%20Conditional%20Statements.md), 10,036 bytes.\\r\\n- [Provenance](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/PROVENANCE.md) and [SHA-256 manifest](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS).\\r\\n- SHA-256: `814c346c022473d0f3ac362060969c2aec76a1fa5ec0a577466e2776ffec9061`.\\r\\n- Publisher attribution: LSAT Demon, supported by first-party links and signup invitation. Individual author, canonical article URL, publication date and actual capture date are unknown. Related linked pages are not evidence of the canonical article URL. Inbox last-write date was 2026-10-03; original retained unchanged.\\r\\n- No matching archived hash/source summary existed before this intake. No local images occur in the clip. Embedded YouTube links were not played, and the browser-extension iframe was not executed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What the article does\\r\\n\\r\\nThe article starts with ordinary conditional language and defines sufficient as enough to establish the other condition under the supplied rule. Its poison/sickness anchor demonstrates that a sufficient condition guarantees the necessary condition without making the reverse inference valid. It asks readers to accept a supplied premise rather than invent real-world exceptions.\\r\\n\\r\\nIts most useful teaching passage supplies a deliberately artificial restaurant-meal/tax-credit rule. It then asks what someone **needs** to do to receive the credit. The tempting answer mistakes a guaranteed route for the only route. Other routes could fit the rule. This distinguishes “enough for the result” from “required for the result” using an ordinary question rather than labels alone.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe article also demonstrates if-clauses at the end of sentences and omitted “then.” Its examples concern crops, a car-payment instruction and ability to sell spiders after cleaning cages. These are evidence of the article\'s method, not recommended student-facing examples.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The supplied six-question structure\\r\\n\\r\\nThe article uses a satirical law-firm/misery conditional. Abstract it as A → B for source analysis:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Question function | Supplied answer, paraphrased | Precise interpretation for adaptation |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| What is sufficient for B? | A. | A establishes B under the supplied rule. |\\r\\n| What is necessary for B? | Unknown. | The rule does not establish that A is required for B. Do not claim absolutely no necessary conditions exist. |\\r\\n| What is sufficient for A? | Unknown. | The rule does not establish that B is enough for A. Do not deny trivial or other independently supplied sufficient conditions. |\\r\\n| What is necessary for A? | B. | A cannot hold while B is false under the rule. |\\r\\n| Which clause is necessary for the other? | The then-clause. | B is required for A. |\\r\\n| Which clause needs the other? | The if-clause. | A needs B. This wording tests the learner\'s understanding from the opposite grammatical perspective. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe distinction between the last two questions is particularly useful: **B is required for A** and **A requires B** describe the same relationship. Ask both naturally without turning the exercise into a vocabulary trick.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Original adaptation authorized for development\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua approved an original raffle example modeled on this teaching pattern: **If you win this raffle, you get a bicycle.** The relationship is supplied for the exercise, not a claim about every raffle. Winning is enough to establish getting a bicycle. Getting a bicycle alone does not establish winning this raffle: receiving one as a gift or buying one could be consistent alternatives.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the alternatives as possible cases, not new premises about a particular person. Scope each question to what this rule establishes. “Does this rule make winning the raffle necessary for getting a bicycle?” is clearer than asking for an unrestricted inventory of every necessary condition for obtaining a bicycle.\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter the roles have been explained, vary the supplied fact and ask what follows, then ask the learner to describe a bicycle recipient who did not win the raffle. Later contraposition can ask whether someone who never gets a bicycle could satisfy the winning condition under this same rule. Avoid ambiguous snapshots such as “doesn\'t have the bicycle yet,” which do not deny eventual receipt. The [existing source map](../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) owns placement. The [selected teaching voice](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) continues to govern prose.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis records an authorized example direction, not a claim that an app revision, assignment or publication has occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Limits and corrections before reuse\\r\\n\\r\\n- Unsupported prevalence claims, scholarship/tax implications, medical assertions and the law-firm caricature are not imported into student material.\\r\\n- The article\'s statement that a then-clause is not sufficient and an if-clause is not necessary is too absolute. A supplied A → B does not establish B → A; another rule could establish it. Name the missing inference rather than declaring the roles impossible.\\r\\n- “Nothing can fill the blank” overstates what is unknown. Restrict the question to the relation between the two named conditions established by this rule.\\r\\n- Distinguish instructions and permission/ability from observed compliance. A car-payment instruction does not prove how someone paid; ability to sell does not prove a sale.\\r\\n- A premise accepted for an exercise need not be causal, chronological or independently plausible. Do not turn every conditional into a physical trigger.\\r\\n- The article supplies examples and teaching advice, not comparative learning-science evidence. Do not claim measured learning gains from adopting its question pattern.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary](lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary.md), [Abstract Conditionals](lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md). These are three distinct clipped sources. Inbox/archive copies of the first two were hash-identical; this third article was previously unarchived.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"206c46","wall_time_seconds":0.4715517,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1558,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: source\\r\\nsubject: LSAT Demon Abstract Conditionals\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-03\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\ntags: [lsat, conditionals, pedagogy]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/LSAT Demon  Abstract Conditionals.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Move from a familiar rule to an abstract relationship\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua supplied this LSAT Demon clip and requested its integration into the Conditional Bootcamp on 2026-10-03. The entire supplied transcript, 0:00 to 9:10, was read. The closing fragments are incomplete. No source URL, speaker identity, local images or video embed are supplied. No unseen boards or missing speech were reconstructed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Preserved evidence\\r\\n\\r\\n- [Exact Markdown clip](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/LSAT%20Demon%20%20Abstract%20Conditionals.md), 6,712 bytes.\\r\\n- [Provenance](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/PROVENANCE.md) and [hash manifest](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS).\\r\\n- SHA-256: `89a4f5266dc577f09ea99de2a21b77c10d7887724d140e823a04c89b9e1129ee`.\\r\\n- Publication and capture dates unknown. Inbox last-write date was 2026-10-03. Archived and ingested 2026-10-03. Original retained unchanged; no matching archived hash or completed source summary found before intake.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What the transcript actually contains\\r\\n\\r\\n| Timestamp | Source content, paraphrased | Scope and use |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 0:44–1:37 | Boston implies Massachusetts. Not being in Boston does not settle whether a person is in Massachusetts. Not being in Massachusetts excludes Boston. | Compare absent sufficient with absent necessary. Use an original familiar relationship. |\\r\\n| 1:37–3:04 | A stipulated psychology/sociology rule can be accepted for the question even if real course registration works differently. The speaker offers several equivalent forms. | Teach students to reason from the supplied rule, without inventing an explanation for why it exists. |\\r\\n| 3:27–3:56 | Swap the conditions and negate both to form the contrapositive. | Reserve the symbolic operation until the concept and notation have been taught. |\\r\\n| 4:19–5:34 | Progress to X → ¬Y and its contrapositive Y → ¬X; paraphrase using only if and unless. | Negative conditions and abstract labels belong after the notation prerequisites. Unless stays in its later language lesson. |\\r\\n| 6:06–7:36 | Critique unsupported conclusions from ¬Y, then use a smoking/health analogy to make the reverse error concrete. | Keep the counterexample technique; replace the medical example and check both proposed conclusions. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are narrated prompts and demonstrations, not a formal supplied question set. The clip names student concerns about abstract rules. It does not provide a bank of standalone questions, choices or a reviewed answer key.\\r\\n\\r\\n## An explicit transcript discrepancy\\r\\n\\r\\nAt 6:06 the transcript criticizes deriving **¬X** from **¬Y** under **X → ¬Y**. At 6:34 it criticizes deriving **X** from **¬Y**. These are different proposed conclusions. Both are unsupported, but the transcript shifts between them. We cannot identify whether this was a speaker correction or transcription error without playback.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe logically correct teaching point is: given X → ¬Y and ¬Y, X could be true or false. Both X/¬Y and ¬X/¬Y satisfy the rule. Preserve the discrepancy as evidence; do not silently attribute a polished, consistent exercise to the source.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Original adaptations for the bootcamp\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the [existing source map](../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) and [selected voice](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md). These are newly authored examples inspired by the teaching functions above, not source quotations or published assignments.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Familiar inclusion:** Every square is a rectangle. A shape is not a square. Can it still be a rectangle? Yes. A rectangle with unequal adjacent sides supplies a possible case. Use only if these shape meanings are clear; the logic must not turn into a geometry test.\\r\\n2. **Stipulated rule:** Every ticket in Drawer R has a blue border. A ticket is in Drawer R. What follows? It has a blue border. The exercise does not need to explain why tickets were sorted this way.\\r\\n3. **Wording comparison:** “Only blue-bordered tickets are in Drawer R” and “A ticket is in Drawer R only if it has a blue border” both express Drawer R → Blue border. Ask learners to choose the matching relationship and explain what the sentence requires.\\r\\n4. **Negative-condition transfer:** If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop. The room is not hosting a workshop. Does that settle whether a film is showing? No. Ask the learner to build two possible rooms: film/no workshop and no film/no workshop. Both fit. This directly demonstrates why the reverse inference fails.\\r\\n5. **Delayed abstract retrieval:** After the negative-condition lesson, show X → ¬Y and ask which fact rules out X. Y does, because X would require ¬Y. Ask separately what ¬Y establishes about X: nothing definite. Keep the two questions distinct.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Do not import these source habits\\r\\n\\r\\n- Saying a rule no longer matters when one trigger is absent. Keep it available for later facts and other inferences.\\r\\n- Profanity, ridicule or dismissive characterizations of a learner\'s reasoning.\\r\\n- Smoking as a universally sufficient condition for an undefined “not healthy.” That analogy introduces vague and contestable content unrelated to learning the inference.\\r\\n- Legacy Logic Games framing, source promotion or unsupported learning guarantees.\\r\\n- The source\'s hurried “the only”/“must” sentence as a clean indicator template. Use a grammatical original and preserve obligation versus compliance.\\r\\n- Unless/without before their transformations have been taught. Keep all topic prerequisites in the existing 13-part progression and later-unit boundaries.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [sufficient/necessary clip](lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary.md), [LSAT Lab supplement](lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md), [selected voice source](conditional-reasoning-voice.md).\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 12.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6f1781","wall_time_seconds":0.4388495,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3698,"output":"# LSAT Lab conditional lesson: complete caption coverage\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2026-10-03 clipped boards resolve the keyword inventory\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s new [clipped source and screenshot review](../../wiki/sources/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md) covers 0:02 through the 10:18 transition and explicitly examines all twelve embedded screenshots. The clip and all twelve PNGs are preserved byte-for-byte with [hashes](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS). This adds actual visual evidence, without claiming a new full audiovisual watch.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final board now resolves the formerly missing keyword list: left has if, when, whenever, the only, all, each, any, every, no; right has ensures, implies, leads to, will, always, only, only if, requires, must, necessitates, depends on, relies on, unless, until, without. These are observed source categories. No and unless/until/without still require separately taught transformations, not import into an ordinary Group 1/2 placement shortcut. Guarantee words also require full sentence interpretation.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe 7:58–10:18 section supplies the immediate opening enrichment: recognize certainty in different forms; restate the left idea as requiring the right idea; compare equivalent expressions; identify the complete attached idea rather than the nearest noun; cluster expressions by function; understand an unfamiliar requirement even without a memorized word. The requirement board and equivalent-expression board are particularly relevant. Use new examples and keep the approved thirteen-part content order.\\r\\n\\r\\nEarlier video sections contain compound contraposition and chaining. They remain reference for later concepts rather than a reason to overload Group 1/2. The source\'s numerical prevalence claim is unsupported by the supplied evidence; the rule-disappears metaphor should become a precise explanation that an absent trigger leaves the other condition unsettled while the rule remains true.\\r\\n\\r\\nResearch date: 2026-10-01. [Conditional Logic | LR](https://app.lsatlab.com/video-library/?videoid=16784), Patrick Tyrrell, published 2020-07-28. Observed Vimeo embed: 438082783. Parent verified player runtime 24:34. Caption source: `raw/sources/lsat-lab-lectures/16784/source.en-US.vtt`, native en-US track. No audio playback or board inspection is claimed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source summary\\r\\n\\r\\nThe lesson begins with rule meaning and four possible fact positions, establishing valid triggering and invalid reversal/negation. Contraposition expands immediately to compound conditions. Chaining then contrasts rule conclusions with factual conclusions, including a partly satisfied compound trigger. Translation follows these operations: certainty, requirements, indicator direction, negative universals, exception language, and biconditionals. Training uses rhetorical inference questions, two explicit early pause tasks, equivalent translations, and a ten-item pause-and-solve worksheet with an indicator reference. The instructor reviews each item’s indicator, diagram direction, negation, and contrapositive. Later items demand interpretation beyond the memorized list and compound negation. The closing prioritizes indicator fluency, contraposition, compound negation, and avoiding invalid moves. Automated feedback, timed practice, mastery checks, and actual LSAT question IDs are absent from the captions. Quantifiers receive a brief translation aside, not a full treatment.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Chronological factual topic ledger\\r\\n\\r\\nTimes identify approximate teaching transitions in overlapping subtitle cues. Entries are topic labels and observed activity metadata.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Time | Topic | Observed training format |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 00:10–00:31 | Objectives | Introduction |\\r\\n| 00:31–01:02 | Arrow; certainty; sufficient/necessary | Concrete rule |\\r\\n| 01:02–01:41 | Positive trigger; absent trigger | Contrasting cases |\\r\\n| 01:41–02:31 | Positive consequent; reversal; flaw | Contrast and warning |\\r\\n| 02:31–02:58 | Absent consequent | Inference question |\\r\\n| 02:58–04:05 | Contraposition; invalid negation | Explanation |\\r\\n| 04:05–04:37 | Compound antecedent; AND/OR negation | Symbolic demonstration |\\r\\n| 04:37–05:14 | Compound consequent contraposition | Explicit pause; worked answer |\\r\\n| 05:14–05:45 | Rule conclusion versus fact conclusion | Task distinction |\\r\\n| 05:45–06:37 | Three-rule chains; intermediate inferences | Worked symbolic chain |\\r\\n| 06:37–07:11 | Disjunctive outcome with eliminating fact | Worked symbolic inference |\\r\\n| 07:11–07:53 | Partly satisfied conjunction; residual conditional | Explicit pause; worked answer |\\r\\n| 07:53–09:05 | Certainty; universals; guarantees; requirements | Recognition and requirement rephrasing |\\r\\n| 09:05–09:29 | Equivalent wording | Pause to inspect slide examples |\\r\\n| 09:29–10:19 | Indicator columns; semantic categories | Reference list |\\r\\n| 10:19–11:45 | Negative universal | Plausibility contrast; alternate translations |\\r\\n| 11:45–12:53 | Negative quantifier rephrasing | Brief comparison |\\r\\n| 12:53–14:49 | Unless/until/without | Two equivalent approaches; simple examples |\\r\\n| 14:49–16:29 | Exception language with compounds | Parentheses; negation; alternate formulation |\\r\\n| 16:29–17:51 | Biconditional indicators | Two arrows versus same-status cases |\\r\\n| 17:51–18:48 | Otherwise; explicit opposite rule | Worked interpretation |\\r\\n| 18:48–19:29 | Ten-item worksheet instructions | Reference aid; pause; translation plus contraposition |\\r\\n| 19:29–19:49 | Item 1: when | Answer review |\\r\\n| 19:49–20:15 | Item 2: implies | Answer review |\\r\\n| 20:15–20:32 | Item 3: no | Answer review |\\r\\n| 20:32–20:49 | Item 4: each | Answer review |\\r\\n| 20:49–21:18 | Item 5: unless | Two approaches |\\r\\n| 21:18–21:53 | Item 6: never | Unlisted-word interpretation |\\r\\n| 21:53–22:19 | Item 7: any; alternative will reading | Scope and ambiguity |\\r\\n| 22:19–22:39 | Item 8: only | Answer review |\\r\\n| 22:39–23:18 | Item 9: without plus AND | Compound negation; alternate approach |\\r\\n| 23:18–23:42 | Item 10: prerequisite | Unlisted-word interpretation |\\r\\n| 23:42–24:20 | Fluency goals | Recap |\\r\\n| 24:20–24:35 | Outro | End material |\\r\\n\\r\\nNo proprietary worksheet sentences or answer bank are reproduced here.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Original analysis: prerequisites and learning demand\\r\\n\\r\\nThis compact lesson assumes the learner can handle abstract letters and elementary negation almost immediately. It introduces AND/OR contraposition approximately four minutes in, before detailed indicator translation. That makes it useful as a compressed overview or refresher; a ground-up independent pathway would benefit from more isolated practice before the combined tasks. This judgment concerns instructional demand, not evidence of learner outcomes.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe four fact-position cases teach an important boundary: from S→N, S licenses N and ¬N licenses ¬S; ¬S and N alone leave the other term undetermined. Students must interpret uncertainty as an answer rather than feel compelled to infer something.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe linking section requires a different output type from triggering. Rules alone yield another relationship. Adding a fact can yield a factual consequence. A fact satisfying part of an AND antecedent can also simplify the remaining conditional demand. This last operation merits its own assignable skill in LSAT U rather than being hidden under general linking.\\r\\n\\r\\nTranslation strategy is partly syntactic and partly semantic. The reference identifies arrow placement, but unlisted words and equivalent requirement readings require understanding necessity. The lesson therefore does not justify teaching indicator memorization as sufficient mastery.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe practice sequence shifts from guided cases to a mixed worksheet and delayed instructor review. This is feedback through worked explanation after the student pauses. Captions provide no evidence of answer entry, immediate adaptive feedback, a scored diagnostic, retries with fresh items, or gating. A ten-item mixed worksheet is one observed training event, not a complete inventory of LSAT Lab exercises.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Original LSAT U examples and assignable skills\\r\\n\\r\\nAll examples below are new and are not paraphrases of source exercises.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Fact positions:** every licensed pilot has passed an exam. Test separately what follows from licensed, not licensed, passed, and not passed.\\r\\n- **Compound contraposition:** inspected AND sealed → dispatchable. If not dispatchable, at least one prerequisite failed. Students must not infer both failed.\\r\\n- **Rule versus fact:** regional → registered; registered → approved. Derive regional → approved. Only after a named applicant is regional may the student derive that applicant’s approval.\\r\\n- **Residual condition:** code AND badge → entry; entry → attendance. Given the badge, code → attendance still remains conditional until a code fact is supplied.\\r\\n- **Equivalent exception translations:** equipment operates only when a safety check has passed. Require both a necessity reading and its contrapositive.\\r\\n- **Biconditional scope:** a display lights exactly when the circuit is active. Contrast an explicitly given two-way rule with an unsupported reversal of a one-way rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Joshua\'s Beginner/Medium/Advanced model, start with a single operation, then mix operations, then vary language and compounds. Use a separate general mixed assignment at each level. These are design proposals, not LSAT Lab\'s observed level definitions.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Nuances and source-fidelity limits\\r\\n\\r\\nThe short negative-quantifier aside includes few, most, and not-all transformations. In LSAT U, define the intended mathematical meaning of few before equating it with most-not; everyday usage can be vague. No existence assumption should be silently imported into a universal rule. The captions do not give a full quantifier inference lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe certainty discussion includes guaranteed causal wording. LSAT U should distinguish the logical guarantee encoded by a sentence from proof of causation in an argument. The lesson\'s broad certainty framing is a recognition heuristic, not a complete account of every kind of statement.\\r\\n\\r\\nOtherwise creates a biconditional in the discussed case because the other branch supplies the opposite outcome. It should not be taught as automatically biconditional in every sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nNative-track labeling does not establish transcription accuracy. Captions contain obvious substitutions involving contrapositive, flaw, letters, names, and ordinary nouns. One compound explanation labels the inferred side inconsistently; diagrams are necessary to verify its intended symbols. The item with alternative any/will readings requires the unseen sentence and subject scope to adjudicate fully. The slide\'s complete indicator list, displayed diagrams, and exact written worksheet were not recovered by reading captions. Do not fill these gaps from guesses.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Coverage check\\r\\n\\r\\nAll 573 numbered cues were read from opening to final cue; the initially truncated middle was read separately. First cue: 00:02.960. Final cue: 24:33.080–24:35.140. Only gaps greater than two seconds are the initial 2.960-second lead-in and 6.030 seconds before the final outro cue. Overlapping cues are normal here. Final timestamp exceeds the observed 24:34 runtime by 1.140 seconds; preserve this mismatch rather than trimming the source.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis establishes complete coverage of the supplied caption stream and its teaching sequence. It does not establish audiovisual fidelity, complete board content, or a complete LSAT Lab curriculum.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2026-10-02 public video review and opening correction\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the local first page: it asked what a rule tells the student before explaining what conditionals are or why they matter. This supersedes acceptance of the earlier opening preview; tests and visual checks did not establish teaching quality.\\r\\n\\r\\nReviewed Patrick\'s official public [Conditional Logic | LSAT Logical Reasoning](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jeDnykOrswY), observed runtime 24:34. Full exported auto-generated English caption stream covers 00:02 through 24:33. Read every caption. A read-only comparison found all 573 caption texts match the earlier archived lesson 16784 exactly. This verifies public-video identity with the prior caption analysis. No separate LSAT Loud title has been established; that wording is treated as a likely dictation reference to LSAT Lab, not verified product metadata.\\r\\n\\r\\nFresh browser visual inspection adds evidence beyond the prior caption-only review:\\r\\n\\r\\n- 01:05: the same NYC/USA rule has a separate Linda fact and a question before the conclusion is shown.\\r\\n- 01:10: the conclusion is revealed beside that fact; the original relationship remains visible.\\r\\n- 01:25: an absent trigger is contrasted with an unknown outcome; the board explicitly permits either outcome.\\r\\n- 02:40: a separate contrapositive board reverses and negates the two terms. This was viewed, not imported into the introductory lesson.\\r\\n- 09:04: a requirement reading and combined requirement chain are on the board.\\r\\n- 09:14: equivalent expression families are grouped on a reference board, following the explicit pause instruction at about 09:05.\\r\\n- 19:19: an indicator reference is available during the pause-and-diagram instructions before answer explanations.\\r\\n\\r\\nScreenshots are in visual-proof/ with the lab-primer prefix and timestamps. The 01:05 filename was initially captured after a keyboard Home shortcut accidentally reset playback; it was corrected by seeking back and verifying both media currentTime 65 and displayed 1:05 before saving. No invalid frame is offered as proof. Source screenshots are private research evidence, not student lesson assets. No full real-time audio watch or precise silence-duration measurement is claimed.\\r\\n\\r\\nExplicit pause requests from the captions occur around 04:39, 07:11, 09:05 and 19:16. Earlier rhetorical questions and staged visual reveals establish question-before-answer progression, not a verified multi-second silent pause.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe design pattern to adapt is one relationship, one changed fact, a learner-controlled thinking interval, then the explanation and a contrasting mistake. The video itself introduces abstract symbols and contraposition quickly. Joshua\'s requested first page should be slower: what a condition means; what a conditional statement connects; why this matters on the LSAT; certainty compared with usual likelihood; one fully explained original example; then a pause before applying it. Sufficient/necessary terminology and indicator families belong after this introduction. The original LSAT U ready/packed example can remain after this foundation, rather than serving as a substitute for the definition.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a626a0","wall_time_seconds":0.9014672,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3522,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"86d95f","wall_time_seconds":0.8640504,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":974,"output":"![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_ho_yxw_Qqc)\\r\\n\\r\\nGet your best score with LSAT Demon. Sign up here: https://www.lsatdemon.com  \\r\\n  \\r\\nAttend over 30 hours of live LSAT classes every week with a Demon Live subscription: https://www.lsatdemon.com/plans  \\r\\n  \\r\\nUse our Law School Scholarship Estimator to see what LSAT score you need to go to law school for free: https://www.lsatdemon.com/scholarships  \\r\\n  \\r\\nMeet the Demon’s amazing LSAT teachers and tutors: https://www.lsatdemon.com/about  \\r\\n  \\r\\nLearn more about Ben, Nathan, and their years of experience teaching the LSAT: https://thinkinglsat.com/about\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transcript\\r\\n\\r\\n**0:04** · conditional reasoning all it really is is just a rule that only applies in certain circumstances so there\'s a rule that\'s not a hard and fast rule it\'s a rule that has a trigger on the rule\\r\\n\\r\\n**0:26** · and there are many ways of phrasing a rule that has a condition on it again we\'re talking about conditional reasoning there are many ways of phrasing a rule with a condition on it but they all mean the same thing you just have to get familiar with the ways that they talk about\\r\\n\\r\\n**0:51** · these conditions so consider this example this pen this dry erase pen if i drop it it will fall yeah everybody agree if i drop it it will fall does that mean it\'s gonna fall for sure\\r\\n\\r\\n**1:16** · no it\'s not falling now because i didn\'t drop it okay so if i drop it it will fall the trigger on the rule the thing that\\r\\n\\r\\n**1:31** · makes happen is when i drop the pin i drop the pen the pen falls right okay so the sufficient condition\\r\\n\\r\\n**1:48** · on that rule by the way sufficient just means enough enough to prove one other thing the trigger on the rule the sufficient condition on the rule is if i drop it if i drop it then it will fall so the sufficient condition is i dropped it the necessary condition is the thing that happens when i drop it if i drop it then it must be true that\\r\\n\\r\\n**2:15** · it The admissions example is illustrative wording, not an empirically verified admissions rule to reuse. |\\r\\nwiki/sources/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md:57:| 060311 | Equivalent wording grouped into four families | If/when/whenever; universals; outcome-guarantee forms; requirement forms express the same relation despite different sentence order. The table includes negative and exception forms outside the simple Group 1/2 scope. |\\r\\nwiki/sources/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md:62:The last board\'s **left** inventory is: if, when, whenever, the only; all, each, any, every, no. Its **right** inventory is: ensures, implies, leads to, will, always; only, only if, requires, must, necessitates, depends on, relies on, unless, until, without. These are the vendor\'s observed labels, not an unconditional parsing algorithm and not Joshua\'s complete group taxonomy.\\r\\nwiki/sources/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md:64:For the ordinary Group 1/2 work, adapt the rule/universal and requirement/guarantee meanings, preserving the full qualified idea attached to an expression. Sentence location does not determine diagram side. In an if sentence, the if-clause may come first or last. In a requirement sentence, the goal can come before the required thing or after it. Teach students to identify what is enough and what that situation requires, then read the whole relationship back.\\r\\nwiki/sources/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md:74:No fresh real-time video watch or audio review occurred in this ingest. No screenshot captures compound AND/OR work or the residual-trigger example directly; those concepts are supported here by the bounded transcript, not a corresponding board capture.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:31:Joshua\'s marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:33:2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same relationship with if at the beginning and in the middle, and show necessary targets both before and after requirement language. Neither sentence position nor the words immediately following the indicator determine placement. Preserve if versus only if as different complete expressions.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:35:A rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:39:Joshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:47:Joshua rejected the recap\'s small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:92:that triggers step four.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:176:**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:197:short diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:214:relationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:215:that the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:246:blurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:260:Joshua rejected “same rule, different words,” “equivalent-rule comparison,” “find the requirement, then place it,” “work through the expressions below,” “first choose the complete target,” “read the whole idea,” and “sentence X of Y” when these stood in for a useful explanation or made the drill feel like a collection of labeled sections. “Complete target” and similar terminology should not carry a beginner\'s instructions without a concrete meaning attached.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:266:Keep modality in the examples. An electric vehicle that is denied permission disproves the claim that being electric guarantees permission; a vehicle merely staying outside does not. A required action is an obligation, not a fact proving compliance. Confirmation practice for that mistake must test another obligation rather than an unrelated wording comparison.\\r\\nwiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md:270:Further steering in the same session: “Keep the complete condition” was itself awkward as a visible heading. Ask who or what the particular rule covers instead. Joshua also rejected the landing page\'s total question/stage counts, per-stage counts, long stage list and padded explanation of repairs. A shorter launch should explain the immediate task; each stage should introduce its purpose and instructions when the learner reaches it. That latest flow revision was requested while final verification was still pending, so this record does not certify its implementation.\\r\\nreferences/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md:29:- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\nreferences/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md:31:- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\nreferences/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md:71:The reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\nreferences/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md:81:**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. It asks readers to accept a supplied premise rather than invent real-world exceptions.\\nwiki/sources/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements.md:28:Its most useful teaching passage supplies a deliberately artificial restaurant-meal/tax-credit rule.
