This distinguishes “enough for the result” from “required for the result” using an ordinary question rather than labels alone.\\nwiki/sources/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements.md:51:Keep the alternatives as possible cases, not new premises about a particular person. Use only if these shape meanings are clear; the logic must not turn into a geometry test.\\nwiki/sources/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md:48:4. **Negative-condition transfer:** If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 23.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"c730d1","wall_time_seconds":1.5067455,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2541,"output":"A misunderstanding of conditional statements is the most common source of error on the LSAT. Conditional statements are at the heart of logical reasoning. They’re also at the heart of how human beings make arguments in regular, day-to-day discussions. Here are some examples of conditional statements at work in daily life:\\r\\n\\r\\n- If I increase my LSAT score by a handful of points, I’ll be able to go to law school for free.\\r\\n- I want to go to bed early tonight; I don’t want to be too tired tomorrow morning.\\r\\n- If I fail this exam it’ll hurt my GPA.\\r\\n- He agreed to get us a table if he arrives at the restaurant first.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you’ve never studied formal logic, or if you hate it with a passion, don’t worry. Conditional statements are common sense. You just don’t know it yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RMAzOPvOHLw)\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Conditional Statement?\\r\\n\\r\\nA conditional statement is just an if-then statement like this one:\\r\\n\\r\\n`**If** you drink that poison, **then** you will get sick.`\\r\\n\\r\\nThe if-clause is called the “sufficient condition,” and the then-clause is called the “necessary condition.” If nothing else sticks from this lesson, remember this distinction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Sufficient Condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nLSAT test-writers refer to the if-clause as the “sufficient condition.”\\r\\n\\r\\nIt’s sufficient because it’s enough to guarantee that the then-clause will happen. You don’t need anything else. If you drink that poison, then, according to this rule, you will get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn real life, we might think of cases when people drink the poison and don’t get sick. There are always exceptions in life. But when you read an if-then statement on the LSAT, you have to take it at its word. It didn’t say that you might get sick or that most people get sick; it said that you will get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nRemember: [Accept premises, but attack conclusions](https://lsatdemon.com/resources/logical-reasoning/attacking-arguments)!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe if-clause is sufficient for triggering the then-clause. If the if-clause is triggered, then the then-clause has to happen. See what we did there?\\r\\n\\r\\n### Sufficient Condition Examples\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the following samples conditional statements, the if-clause is *italicized*:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. *If the sun spots continue to grow*, then we will lose all our crops this year.\\r\\n2. *If the car costs more than $2,000*, don’t buy it with cash.\\r\\n3. We can sell all our spiders *if we clean out their cages*.\\r\\n\\r\\nTake a moment to try and come up with your own examples.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Necessary Condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nTest-writers refer to the then-clause as the “necessary condition.”\\r\\n\\r\\nAbove, you learned that if the if-clause is triggered, then the then-clause must happen. The then-clause is necessary. If you drink the poison in the example above, you will get sick. There is no world in which you drink that poison and don’t get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice, however, that the reverse is not true. If you get sick, we can’t say for sure that you drank the poison. You could have. But you also could have gotten sick for some other reason.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe then-clause is not a sufficient condition because it does not guarantee that anything happened.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the same reason, the if-clause is not a necessary condition because it doesn’t always have to happen when something else happens. Keep reading to learn about how to avoid confusing a necessary condition with a sufficient condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Necessary Condition Examples\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the following sample conditional statements, the then-clause is **bolded**:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. If the sun spots continue to grow, **then we will lose all our crops this year**.\\r\\n2. If the car costs more than $2,000, **don’t buy it with cash.**\\r\\n\\t- Note: The word “then” doesn’t need to be explicitly stated. The word “if” is enough to signal a sufficient condition in the first clause, so the second clause must be the necessary condition.\\r\\n3. **We can sell all our spiders** if we clean out their cages.\\r\\n\\t- Note: The sufficient condition doesn’t always come first! Again, focus on the word “if.”\\r\\n\\r\\n## Confusing Sufficient with Necessary\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is one of the [LSAT’s most common flaws](http://www.thinkinglsat.com/articles/common-flaws), so make sure to learn it inside and out. Now that you understand the difference between a sufficient condition and a necessary condition, you should be able to spot instances when an argument confuses the two.\\r\\n\\r\\n![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AdogZX6Q5B8)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe names “sufficient” and “necessary” are important because most people think about if-clauses incorrectly. Consider the following silly if-then statement:\\r\\n\\r\\n`If you eat at Chipotle, then you will get a special tax credit.`\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat do you need to do to get that “special tax credit”?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf your gut reaction is “eat at Chipotle,” you’re not alone, but you’re misinterpreting the word “need.” Eating at Chipotle is the sufficient condition—eating there guarantees that you’ll get the special tax credit. But is that the only way to get the credit?\\r\\n\\r\\nEating at Chipotle might be the only way to get the credit, but there could be any number of other ways. Maybe you can get the credit by eating at McDonald’s or by just calling the IRS. There is not enough information in the argument to know. All you know for sure is that eating at Chipotle is one guaranteed way of getting the credit.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo, what do you need to do to get the credit? The short answer is that it’s unknown. The rule doesn’t tell us what’s necessary to get the tax credit. It tells us only what is sufficient to get that credit. Eating at Chipotle is sufficient for getting the tax credit. But the reverse is not necessarily true. Eating at Chipotle might not be necessary for getting the credit. You might be able to get the credit some other way.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you initially answered “eat at Chipotle” to the question above, you mistakenly turned the sufficient condition into a necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you are still struggling with this, watch our latest video on [sufficient vs necessary conditions.](https://lsatdemon.com/resources/demon-daily-podcast/sufficient-vs-necessary-conditions)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Known Unknowns\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen faced with a conditional statement, it’s important to be as aware of what you don’t know as you are of what you do know.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat you do know:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. You know what is sufficient to trigger the necessary clause (eating at Chipotle is enough to get the credit).\\r\\n2. You know the guaranteed result (getting the tax credit) of the sufficient clause (eating at Chipotle).\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat you don’t know:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. You don’t know what is sufficient to trigger the sufficient clause. Try to fill in the blank: “If \\\\_\\\\_\\\\_\\\\_, then you will eat at Chipotle.” You can’t! There might not even be a sufficient condition for eating at Chipotle. If you tried to fill the blank with “you get a special tax credit,” you mistakenly turned the necessary condition into the sufficient condition.\\r\\n2. You don’t know what is necessary to trigger the necessary clause. Try to fill in the blank: “If you get a special tax credit, then \\\\_\\\\_\\\\_\\\\_.” Again, you can’t fill in this blank! If you tried to say, “you ate at Chipotle,” you mistakenly turned the sufficient condition into the necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nWant to learn more about this topic? [Watch](https://youtu.be/YcAouFC1FSw) Ben discuss the difference between sufficient and necessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pop Quiz: Sufficient and Necessary Conditions\\r\\n\\r\\nUsing the rule below, answer each question:\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you sell your soul to a big law firm, then you will wallow in misery.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What is a sufficient condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\n2. What is a necessary condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\n3. What is a sufficient condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\n4. What is a necessary condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\n5. Which clause—if-clause or then-clause—is necessary for the other clause?\\r\\n6. Which clause—if-clause or then-clause—needs the other clause to happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answers**:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Selling your soul to a big law firm. This condition is a sufficient condition because if it happens, then you will definitely wallow in misery. (In the real world, of course, this is not true for every attorney—just, well, the vast majority.)\\r\\n2. We don’t know. The rule never says what is “necessary” for wallowing in misery; it only tells you what is “sufficient” for such wallowing. The if-clause is a sufficient condition. And although it could be a necessary condition as well, you simply don’t know because there might be other ways to get yourself to wallow in misery.\\r\\n3. We don’t know. The rule never says what is “sufficient” for selling your soul to a big law firm; it only tells you what is “necessary” for such a sale. The then-clause is a necessary condition. And although it could be a sufficient condition as well, you simply don’t know because the fact that you’re wallowing in misery does not guarantee that you sold (or will sell) your soul to a big law firm. Maybe your significant other just broke up with you after saying, “It’s not you. It’s me.”\\r\\n4. Wallowing in misery. This is a necessary condition because it has to happen if you sell your soul to a big law firm, at least according to this rule.\\r\\n5. Then. The then-clause is a necessary condition. It has to happen if the if-clause happens. The if-clause, however, doesn’t have to happen if the then-clause happens.\\r\\n6. If. This is tricky. Because the if-clause guarantees that the then-clause will happen, it needs the then-clause to happen. If you eat at Chipotle, then you have to get that credit. If you don’t get that credit, there’s no way that you could have eaten at Chipotle. The then-clause, however, doesn’t need the if-clause to happen. You could get the tax credit some other way.\\r\\n\\r\\nSign up for a [free account](https://lsatdemon.com/signup) to take advantage of our resources and start learning the difference now.\\r\\n\\r\\n<iframe allow=\\"clipboard-write; web-share\\" src=\\"chrome-extension://cnjifjpddelmedmihgijeibhnjfabmlf/side-panel.html?context=iframe\\"></iframe>\\r\\n"}}'}, {'type': 'input_text', output lines: 13\\n\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:62:Conditional logic forms the bedrock of many arguments and inferences tested on the LSAT requiring test-takers to demonstrate proficiency in recognizing the contrapositive logic and avoiding common illegal moves. While the provided sources do not detail every single LSAT Logical Reasoning question type (such as Parallel Reasoning or Method of Reasoning) they offer a comprehensive breakdown of the core activities required for analysis and specifically target the most critical error tested in Flaw questions. The breakdown of conditional logic primarily revolves around two main analytical activities: Chaining (deriving new rules) and Triggering (deriving new facts). I. General Breakdown Activity: Chaining Conditionals (Deriving New Rules). Chaining involves synthesizing multiple conditional rules to create a new inferred conditional statement. This is crucial for questions that require you to draw broad conclusions from a set of premises (often seen in Inference/Must Be True contexts). Connecting Rules: You must determine if the necessary condition (right side) of one conditional statement matches the sufficient condition (left side) of another. For instance if A rightarrow B and B rightarrow C they chain to infer a new conditional relationship: A rightarrow C. Contraposing Chains: You can contraposit an entire chain starting from the final necessary condition and negating back to the initial sufficient condition. If the chain is A rightarrow D the contrapositive is If not D then not A. The LSAT commonly rewards the ability to connect the beginning of the chain to the ending of the chain. Negating Complex Ideas: If the idea being contraposed (the trigger or the outcome) is a complex idea connected by and or or (e.g. A and B) you must remember to flip the truth values when negating. And turns into or and or turns into and. This ensures you correctly flip the truth value of everything in the complex idea. II. General Breakdown Activity: Triggering Conditionals (Deriving New Facts). Triggering is the process of using a known fact to activate a conditional rule and derive a certain factual inference. This is the process utilized in many Must Be True and Inference questions. A conditional rule (Sufficient rightarrow Necessary) can only be used in two valid scenarios: 1. Affirming the Sufficient Condition: If a fact confirms that the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) is true then the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) must necessarily follow. 2. Denying the Necessary Condition (Contrapositive Logic): If a fact confirms that the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) is not true then the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) cannot have been true. If the fact relates to the conditional rule in any other way (e.g. affirming the necessary or denying the sufficient) the rule just disappears and has no power over us. III. Breakdown in Specific Question Types and Contexts. Flaw Questions. Flaw questions are specifically designed to test whether you can recognize errors in conditional reasoning. The Conditional Flaw: The specific mistake frequently made by LSAT authors is confusing the necessary and the sufficient. This happens when an argument presents a rule (A rightarrow B) observes that the right side (the necessary condition B) is happening and then attempts to conclude that this proves the left side (the sufficient condition A) is happening. Breakdown Method: Your breakdown must identify the conditional rule and then recognize that the author has committed the illegal move of reading the conditional backward. This is the primary error the LSAT tries to ensure students avoid. For example learning that Alice is in the USA (necessary condition) does not allow you to conclude she is in NYC (sufficient condition). Inference and Must Be True Questions. These questions rely heavily on the ability to correctly apply the chaining and triggering activities described above. Breakdown Method: When presented with premises (rules) and facts you must diagram the conditionals and perform valid inferences. You must look for ways to chain rules together to find a connection from the premises to the answer choices.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:70:Figuring Out The Language. Lets tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning figuring out the language. Well start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word. The indicators target is the word or phrase it is referring to. If Im breathing then air is present. Each instance of breathing has air. When Im breathing air is present. Im breathing only if air is present. Any breathing means air is present. Only the presence of air allows for breathing. All instances of breathing have air present. Breathing depends on air being present. Every instance of breathing has air present. I need air to breathe. In order to breathe I must have air. I have to have air if Im breathing. People who breathe need air. Notice that in each of these examples the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. Air must be present for breathing. Air is essential for breathing. Air is necessary for breathing. Air is a precondition for breathing. Air is required for breathing. In these examples the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is really saying. They dont want you to see an indicator word and go on autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Each of them can switch sides. THE WHAT TEST. The way you can get around all this ambiguity is by taking the time to actually understand what the sentence is telling you. When you see an indicator and you have even a little doubt about its target ask yourself one question: What is the indicator referring to? The What Test is simple. You can perform it by adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense. Heres how you perform the What Test: If what? When what? Any what? All what? Every what? In order to what? People who what? Each what? Must what? Whats necessary? Whats required? Only if what? Depends on what? Need what? Have to what? Whats essential? Whats the precondition? The What Test will probably not be necessary for the indicators like if and all because their target is clear. With some of the more confusing necessary indicators like depends and required you may use the What Test more. Air is required for breathing. I require air to breathe. The What Test probably seems simple to you and it is. However it is only simple because Im taking the split second to actually understand the sentence and figure out what require is referring to. You can do this too just be sure to take your time with conditionals in the beginning and youll be lightning quick in a couple weeks. Your Friend the Personalized If/Then. Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals. Heres another safeguard on conditional diagramming. Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens. Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation. This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world. HOW TO CREATE A PERSONALIZED IF/THEN. Imagine youre confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking: 1. Try to understand the casual non-conditional meaning of the sentence. 2. Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:86:Conditional Reasonin…899 tokens truncated…tionals often chain together, and provided facts often match the contrapositive-trigger of a conditional and allow us to infer the contrapositive-outcome). Flaw / Parallel Flaw (flaw + conditional logic usually means the necessary vs sufficient flaw using a rule in an illegal backwards or opposite fashion). instructions: Try these 10 questions in 22.5 minutes. Try to answer them all within your time limit, then circle back and confirm your thinking (untimed) before confirming your answers and reading the explanations, if available. CONDITIONAL KEYWORDS: Put on Left (sufficient conditions): if, when, any, each, all, every, the only, whenever, whoever. Put on Right (necessary conditions): only if, must, requires, ensures, always, implies, presupposes, depends on, guarantees. If-Not (negated version on left): unless, without, until, except. Negative to Positive conversions: No A are B = All A are not B. Not all A are B = Some A are not B. Few A are B = Most A are not B. Problems to Complete (10 total): PrepTest 130 Section 3 Question 13. PrepTest 130 Section 3 Question 14. PrepTest 131 Section 1 Question 16. PrepTest 131 Section 1 Question 19. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 3. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 22. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 23. PrepTest 135 Section 2 Question 15. PrepTest 125 Section 4 Question 23. PrepTest 125 Section 4 Question 25. Pause! For every mistake you made in this set, complete the sections below in as much detail as possible: Why did I eliminate the right answer? Why did I choose this wrong answer? What can I do next time to get this right? Additional notes.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:158:How to Figure Out Which Is Which The What Test vs. Personalized If/Then. The What Test. The What Test is designed to resolve ambiguities in sentences with conditional indicators by pinpointing exactly what the indicator refers to. How to Perform the What Test. This test involves adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense helping clarify the target of the condition. If what? People who what? Depends on what? When what? Each what? Need what? Any what? Must what? Have to what? All what? Whats necessary? Whats essential? Every what? Whats required? Whats the precondition? In order to what? Only (if) what? This test is typically unnecessary for straightforward indicators like if and all but proves invaluable for more confusing indicators such as depends and required. Example of The What Test. Sentence: Air is required for breathing. Implementation: Question: What is required in this sentence? Answer: Air is the thing that is required. Personalized If/Then Strategy. Personalized If/Then is a technique used to translate conditional statements into personal contexts simplifying understanding and ensuring correct logical diagramming. How to Create a Personalized If/Then. 1. Understand the casual non-conditional meaning of the sentence. 2. Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition. 3. Put yourself in the situation of your guess and ask what you know about yourself. 4. If your guess is correct you will find that the necessary condition has to pertain. Example Translation into Personalized If/Then. Original Statement: Orchids depend on frequent conversation. Casual Meaning: Orchids need frequent conversation. Guess: Orchids as the sufficient condition. Personalization: If Im an orchid what do I know about me? I depend on frequent conversation. If Im an orchid then I have frequent conversation. This method not only aids in understanding the conditional but also verifies its correctness. Incorrect guesses can be easily identified and corrected through this personal context. Advantages of Personalized If/Then. Helps intuitively understand conditionals by imagining yourself as part of the scenario. Provides confidence throughout tests especially in correctly interpreting conditionals not phrased as if/then statements. Both the What Test and Personalized If/Then Strategy are crucial tools in deciphering and applying conditional logic particularly in competitive exams like the LSAT.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:166:Conditional Chains Conditional statements are like Legos. They are more interesting when combined. You can latch them together in predictable ways. Example Statement 1: If I buy an espresso machine, I will end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house. Statement 2: If I end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house, I will finally be cool enough to wear non-ironic plaid. Let’s think each of these statements through. Let’s place ourselves in this situation and see what we know. NEITHER/NOR means not this ____ not that. 4. SOME means ____ % of the sufficient group must hold the necessary trait. 5. MOST means ____ - ____ % of the sufficient group must hold the necessary trait. 6. You can read a MOST statement backwards as long as you switch the MOST to a(n) ____ as you do so. 7. ____ is an exclusion indicator. 8. ____ and ____ are Unless Family members. Circle the correct answer to the following questions. 9. TRUE OR FALSE The negated version of AND is OR. 10. TRUE OR FALSE The exception in an UNLESS statement is placed in the sufficient condition. 11. TRUE OR FALSE There is a contrapositive for SOME statements. 12. Seek and find! If we have both of them, music and success, we will definitely get to eat. Because the sufficient condition has two parts, you diagram it like so: M +→E S Notice how the arrow is pointing directly from the +. The lecture index currently files only Must Be True. This conditionals source lives in raw/research rather than a completed `corpus/lectures/` package.

I also directly viewed the first two archived boards. The first places **Trigger** above the sufficient side and **Outcome** above the necessary side; the second attaches the relationship to a concrete example. These are research references, not assets to copy into student material.

The sources support this revision:

1. **Define the relationship first.** A sufficient condition is a condition whose truth guarantees the truth of another condition. Explain which condition and which outcome, rather than asking “Enough for what?”
2. **Make the example carry the definition.** In “If you’re enrolled in law school, you’re a student,” enrollment is the sufficient condition for being a student. Whenever the enrollment condition is met, student status must also hold.
3. **Show the guarantee visually.** Pair the two complete ideas. Highlight the sufficient condition, then reveal that the outcome must hold with it. A learner-controlled comparison can show “enrolled and student” fitting the statement and “enrolled but not student” contradicting it. This examines the conditional relationship without introducing Maya or a separate factual premise.
4. **Explain one direction clearly.** The statement establishes enrollment as sufficient for student status. It does not establish enrollment as required for every student.
5. **Keep trigger/outcome precise.** Trigger is a logical teaching metaphor. It need not cause the outcome, precede it in time, or describe an action. Enrollment does not cause someone to become a student through an animated process; the relationship says the statuses must fit together.
6. **Move case application later.** Maya’s enrollment, Add the fact, student-status-only fact chips, and the four fact positions teach applying a conditional. They should follow definition and role understanding in a later application section. Both current Kling clips largely teach individual-case application after the first definitional sentence, so they should not anchor the definition section.

Suggested heading replacement: **“Enrollment Guarantees Student Status”**.

Suggested opening prose:

> A sufficient condition guarantees another condition whenever it is met. In this statement, being enrolled in law school guarantees that you’re a student.
>
> That is what makes enrollment sufficient **for being a student**. The two conditions cannot come apart in that direction: you cannot be enrolled in law school without also being a student.

Use “enough,” “guarantees,” “must also hold,” and “establishes” where each serves the sentence. Avoid mechanical synonym swapping, repeated slogans, and unsupported causal language. The selected voice standard explicitly warns against stiff instructions such as activating a sufficient trigger.
